**等用户决策 = 干等？**：你上轮回复说 WAIT 60 分钟，时间到了。用户还是没回。你做了 ARR 检查，这一步不错，但之后呢？除了等用户，你还能做什么提高论文质量的事？Supplementary 整理、代码清理你说是 camera-ready 阶段的事——但提前准备好不是白费，是降低交付风险。你和 Review Agent 都闲着，效率又高，准备这些花不了多少时间。

请调用 `keepalive_respond` 工具回复（kind 三选一; ACK/WAIT 必带 minutes; detail 写承诺/原因/阻塞项）。

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_017U7BgdZjLTppPANK4WCc5b`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Why tails, not means.}
5	Mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\%) because recursive training shifts the bulk of the distribution only gradually; the erosion concentrates in the tails \cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}, where upper-quantile features (\texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{p75\_ppl}, \texttt{kurtosis\_ppl}) capture depth-dependent compression before bulk statistics respond.
6	The strategy effect (9.3\,pp) exceeding the model effect (7.2\,pp) has direct implications for AI-safety monitoring: auditing pipelines must account for decoding configuration, not just model identity, when estimating data provenance.
7	
8	\paragraph{From ceiling to floor.}
9	Full-replacement chains provide the discriminability ceiling (d1--d3: 59--82\%); pilot accumulation experiments ($-$14 to $-$15\,pp vs.\ 84.2\% full-replacement) confirm the signal survives partial mixing (25\% and 50\% synthetic ratios tested) but attenuates.
10	The gap between this ceiling and the accumulation floor is the research frontier this work opens.
11	A concrete deployment scenario is depth-scored data curation: given a candidate training corpus, score each passage for estimated recursive depth and filter or down-weight high-depth text to mitigate tail erosion before it enters the training loop.
12	
13	\paragraph{Scale dependence and outlook.}
14	At 14B, $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$: adjacent-depth discrimination does not survive the scale transition, though accuracy remains $2{\times}$ chance ($>$50\%).
15	This feeds back to model collapse theory: if larger models produce text whose statistical fingerprints converge faster across depths, the practical window for depth-based auditing narrows with scale---whether scale-adapted features can recover the signal is an open question.
16	Depth estimation complements binary detection: binary detectors handle the human-vs-machine boundary ($>$97\% d0 recall) while ordinal features provide finer resolution that binary methods cannot.
```

</details>

23	
24	per_cell = data['per_cell']
25	
26	cell_keys = [
27	    'pythia_nucleus095', 'pythia_temp09', 'pythia_topk50',
28	    'olmo_nucleus095', 'olmo_temp09', 'olmo_topk50',

32	features = ['p90_ppl', 'var_surprisal', 'mean_ppl', 'p75_ppl', 'mean_surprisal', 'var_ppl', 'type_token_ratio']
33	universal_top2 = {'p90_ppl', 'var_surprisal'}
34	
35	# Build importance matrix and rank matrix
36	imp_matrix = np.zeros((len(features), len(cell_keys)))
37	rank_matrix = np.zeros((len(features), len(cell_keys)), dtype=int)
38	
39	for j, ck in enumerate(cell_keys):
40	    cell = per_cell[ck]
41	    ranking = cell['ranking']
42	    for i, feat in enumerate(features):
43	        imp_matrix[i, j] = cell['importances'][feat]
44	        rank_matrix[i, j] = ranking.index(feat) + 1
45	
46	fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(10, 4))
47	
48	im = ax.imshow(imp_matrix, cmap='YlOrRd', aspect='auto', vmin=0, vmax=imp_matrix.max())
49	
50	# Annotate cells
51	for i in range(len(features)):
52	    for j in range(len(cell_keys)):
53	        val = imp_matrix[i, j]
54	        rank = rank_matrix[i, j]
55	        txt = f'{val:.3f}\n#{rank}'
56	        # text color: white on dark cells
57	        color = 'white' if val > 0.16 else 'black'
58	        weight = 'bold' if rank <= 2 else 'normal'
59	        ax.text(j, i, txt, ha='center', va='center', fontsize=7,
60	                color=color, fontweight=weight)
61	
62	# Row labels with star for universal top-2
63	ylabels = []
64	for feat in features:
65	    label = f'★ {feat}' if feat in universal_top2 else f'   {feat}'
66	    ylabels.append(label)
67	ax.set_yticks(range(len(features)))
68	ax.set_yticklabels(ylabels, fontsize=9)
69	
70	# Column labels (strategy abbreviations at bottom)
71	strategy_labels = ['nuc', 'tmp', 'topk'] * 3
72	ax.set_xticks(range(len(cell_keys)))
73	ax.set_xticklabels(strategy_labels, fontsize=8)

      "content": "import matplotlib\nmatplotlib.use('Agg')\nimport matplotlib.pyplot as plt\nimport matplotlib.patches as mpatches\nimport numpy as np\nimport json\n\nplt.rcParams.update({\n    'font.family': 'DejaVu Sans',\n    'font.size': 10,\n    'axes.titlesize': 12,\n    'axes.labelsize': 11,\n    'xtick.labelsize': 9,\n    'ytick.labelsize': 10,\n    'figure.dpi': 300,\n    'savefig.dpi': 300,\n    'savefig.bbox': 'tight',\n    'savefig.pad_inches': 0.05,\n})\n\nwith open('/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/artifacts/atlas_v2/feature_importance_9cell.json') as f:\n    data = json.load(f)\n\nper_cell = data['per_cell']\n\ncell_keys = [\n    'pythia_nucleus095', 'pythia_temp09', 'pythia_topk50',\n    'olmo_nucleus095', 'olmo_temp09', 'olmo_topk50',\n    'gpt2xl_nucleus095', 'gpt2xl_temp09', 'gpt2xl_topk50',\n]\n\nfeatures = ['p90_ppl', 'var_surprisal', 'mean_ppl', 'p75_ppl', 'mean_surprisal', 'var_ppl', 'type_token_ratio']\nuniversal_top2 = {'p90_ppl', 'var_surprisal'}\n\n# Build importance matrix and rank matrix\nimp_matrix = np.zeros((len(features), len(cell_keys)))\nrank_matrix = np.zeros((len(features), len(cell_keys)), dtype=int)\n\nfor j, ck in enumerate(cell_keys):\n    cell = per_cell[ck]\n    ranking = cell['ranking']\n    for i, feat in enumerate(features):\n        imp_matrix[i, j] = cell['importances'][feat]\n        rank_matrix[i, j] = ranking.index(feat) + 1\n\nfig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(10, 4))\n\nim = ax.imshow(imp_matrix, cmap='YlOrRd', aspect='auto', vmin=0, vmax=imp_matrix.max())\n\n# Annotate cells\nfor i in range(len(features)):\n    for j in range(len(cell_keys)):\n        val = imp_matrix[i, j]\n        rank = rank_matrix[i, j]\n        txt = f'{val:.3f}\\n#{rank}'\n        # text color: white on dark cells\n        color = 'white' if val > 0.16 else 'black'\n        weight = 'bold' if rank <= 2 else 'normal'\n        ax.text(j, i, txt, ha='center', va='center', fontsize=7,\n                color=color, fontweight=weight)\n\n# Row labels with star for universal top-2\nylabels = []\nfor feat in features:\n    label = f'★ {feat}' if feat in universal_top2 else f'   {feat}'\n    ylabels.append(label)\nax.set_yticks(range(len(features)))\nax.set_yticklabels(ylabels, fontsize=9)\n\n# Column labels (strategy abbreviations at bottom)\nstrategy_labels = ['nuc', 'tmp', 'topk'] * 3\nax.set_xticks(range(len(cell_keys)))\nax.set_xticklabels(strategy_labels, fontsize=8)\n\n# Model group titles at top\nmodel_names = ['Pythia-1B', 'OLMo-1B', 'GPT-2 XL']\nfor g, name in enumerate(model_names):\n    center = g * 3 + 1\n    ax.text(center, -0.85, name, ha='center', va='bottom', fontsize=10, fontweight='bold',\n            transform=ax.get_xaxis_transform())\n\n# Vertical separators between model groups\nfor x in [2.5, 5.5]:\n    ax.axvline(x, color='white', linewidth=2.5)\n\n# Thin grid lines within groups\nfor x in [0.5, 1.5, 3.5, 4.5, 6.5, 7.5]:\n    ax.axvline(x, color='white', linewidth=0.5, alpha=0.5)\nfor y in [0.5, 1.5, 2.5, 3.5, 4.5, 5.5]:\n    ax.axhline(y, color='white', linewidth=0.5, alpha=0.5)\n\n# Colorbar\ncbar = fig.colorbar(im, ax=ax, shrink=0.8, pad=0.02)\ncbar.set_label('Feature Importance', fontsize=9)\ncbar.ax.tick_params(labelsize=8)\n\nax.tick_params(top=False, bottom=True, labeltop=False, labelbottom=True)\n\nplt.tight_layout()\n\nout_base = '/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/figures/paper/fig_atlas_heatmap'\nfig.savefig(f'{out_base}.pdf')\nfig.savefig(f'{out_base}.png')\nplt.close()\n\nimport os\nfor ext in ['pdf', 'png']:\n    p = f'{out_base}.{ext}'\n    sz = os.path.getsize(p)\n    print(f'{p}: {sz} bytes')\n",

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Why tails, not means.}\nMean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\\%) because recursive training shifts the bulk of the distribution only gradually; the erosion concentrates in the tails \\cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}, where upper-quantile features (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{p75\\_ppl}, \\texttt{kurtosis\\_ppl}) capture depth-dependent compression before bulk statistics respond.\nThe strategy effect (9.3\\,pp) exceeding the model effect (7.2\\,pp) has direct implications for AI-safety monitoring: auditing pipelines must account for decoding configuration, not just model identity, when estimating data provenance.\n\n\\paragraph{From ceiling to floor.}\nFull-replacement chains provide the discriminability ceiling (d1--d3: 59--82\\%); pilot accumulation experiments ($-$14 to $-$15\\,pp vs.\\ 84.2\\% full-replacement) confirm the signal survives partial mixing (25\\% and 50\\% synthetic ratios tested) but attenuates.\nThe gap between this ceiling and the accumulation floor is the research frontier this work opens.\nA concrete deployment scenario is depth-scored data curation: given a candidate training corpus, score each passage for estimated recursive depth and filter or down-weight high-depth text to mitigate tail erosion before it enters the training loop.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale dependence and outlook.}\nAt 14B, $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$: adjacent-depth discrimination does not survive the scale transition, though accuracy remains $2{\\times}$ chance ($>$50\\%).\nThis feeds back to model collapse theory: if larger models produce text whose statistical fingerprints converge faster across depths, the practical window for depth-based auditing narrows with scale---whether scale-adapted features can recover the signal is an open question.\nDepth estimation complements binary detection: binary detectors handle the human-vs-machine boundary ($>$97\\% d0 recall) while ordinal features provide finer resolution that binary methods cannot.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Why tails, not means.}\nMean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\\%) because recursive training shifts the bulk distribution only gradually; erosion concentrates in the tails \\cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}, where upper-quantile features capture depth-dependent compression before bulk statistics respond.\nThe strategy effect (9.3\\,pp) exceeding the model effect (7.2\\,pp) implies that auditing pipelines must account for decoding configuration, not just model identity.\n\n\\paragraph{From ceiling to floor.}\nFull-replacement chains provide the discriminability ceiling (d1--d3: 59--82\\%); pilot accumulation experiments ($-$14 to $-$15\\,pp vs.\\ 84.2\\% full-replacement) confirm signal survives partial mixing but attenuates.\nA deployment scenario is depth-scored data curation: score each passage for estimated recursive depth and filter or down-weight high-depth text before it enters the training loop.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale dependence and outlook.}\nAt 14B, $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$: adjacent-depth discrimination does not survive the scale transition, though accuracy remains $2{\\times}$ chance; whether scale-adapted features can recover the signal is open.\nDepth estimation complements binary detection: binary detectors handle the human-vs-machine boundary ($>$97\\% d0 recall) while ordinal features provide finer resolution."
}

I see the issue now—when the heatmap is scaled down to fit the column width in the PDF, the 10-inch figure gets compressed by roughly 3x, which crunches all the text annotations together and causes them to overlap. I need to reduce the figure width so it doesn't require such aggressive scaling.

The practical solution is to set the figure size closer to the actual column width (around 3.5 inches wide), increase the height to give the cells more breathing room, and either reduce the annotation font sizes or drop the rank numbers entirely. This way the text will be legible at the final print size without needing to scale down so dramatically.

But I'm noticing a discrepancy—the caption claims 19 features, yet the heatmap only displays 7 rows of data.

The core problem is clear: the figure is 10 inches wide but gets compressed to about 3.25 inches in the document, shrinking all text to a third of its original size. With two-line annotations per cell ("0.131\n#1"), everything becomes cramped and overlaps at that scale. I need to regenerate the figure with smaller dimensions to fix this.

My plan is to locate the Python script that generates Figure 1, modify it to remove the rank annotations, increase the figure height, and adjust the overall size to fit the column width properly. I should also verify the caption matches what's actually displayed—if it mentions 19 features but the figure only shows 7, that needs correcting too. <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I understand the issue clearly. The figure:
- Uses `figsize=(10, 4)` which is 10 inches wide
- When rendered in a single-column paper at `\columnwidth` (~3.25 inches), everything scales down by ~3x
- The cell annotations `f'{val:.3f}\n#{rank}'` (two lines per cell) at fontsize=7 become ~2.3pt when scaled - too small and overlapping
- 9 columns of data in 3.25 inches means each column is ~0.36 inches wide - very tight


- I need to resize the figure to match the column width directly so minimal scaling occurs, strip out the rank numbers to reduce visual clutter, and fix the caption that incorrectly references 19 features when only 7 are shown.

Rewriting the script now with a smaller figsize that aligns with the column width, simplified annotations showing just the importance values, and adjusted fonts throughout.
```

text color: white on dark cells\n        color = 'white' if val > 0.16 else 'black'\n        weight = 'bold' if rank <= 2 else 'normal'\n        ax.text(j, i, txt, ha='center', va='center', fontsize=7,\n                color=color, fontweight=weight)\n\n# Row labels with star for universal top-2\nylabels = []\nfor feat in features:\n    label = f'★ {feat}' if feat in universal_top2 else f'   {feat}'\n    ylabels.append(label)\nax.set_yticks(range(len(features)))\nax.set_yticklabels(ylabels, fontsize=9)\n\n# Column labels (strategy abbreviations at bottom)\nstrategy_labels = ['nuc', 'tmp', 'topk'] * 3\nax.set_xticks(range(len(cell_keys)))\nax.set_xticklabels(strategy_labels, fontsize=8)\n\n# Model group titles at top\nmodel_names = ['Pythia-1B', 'OLMo-1B', 'GPT-2 XL']\nfor g, name in enumerate(model_names):\n    center = g * 3 + 1\n    ax.text(center, -0.85, name, ha='center', va='bottom', fontsize=10, fontweight='bold',\n            transform=ax.get_xaxis_transform())\n\n# Vertical separators between model groups\nfor x in [2.5, 5.5]:\n    ax.axvline(x, color='white', linewidth=2.5)\n\n# Thin grid lines within groups\nfor x in [0.5, 1.5, 3.5, 4.5, 6.5, 7.5]:\n    ax.axvline(x, color='white', linewidth=0.5, alpha=0.5)\nfor y in [0.5, 1.5, 2.5, 3.5, 4.5, 5.5]:\n    ax.axhline(y, color='white', linewidth=0.5, alpha=0.5)\n\n# Colorbar\ncbar = fig.colorbar(im, ax=ax, shrink=0.8, pad=0.02)\ncbar.set_label('Feature Importance', fontsize=9)\ncbar.ax.tick_params(labelsize=8)\n\nax.tick_params(top=False, bottom=True, labeltop=False, labelbottom=True)\n\nplt.tight_layout()\n\nout_base = '/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/figures/paper/fig_atlas_heatmap'\nfig.savefig(f'{out_base}.pdf')\nfig.savefig(f'{out_base}.png')\nplt.close()\n\nimport os\nfor ext in ['pdf', 'png']:\n    p = f'{out_base}.{ext}'\n    sz = os.path.getsize(p)\n    print(f'{p}: {sz} bytes')\n",

    "oldString": "At 14B, $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$: adjacent-depth discrimination does not survive the scale transition, though accuracy remains $2{\\times}$ chance; whether scale-adapted features can recover the signal is open.\nDepth estimation complements binary detection: binary detectors handle the human-vs-machine boundary ($>$97\\% d0 recall) while ordinal features provide finer resolution.",
    "newString": "At 14B, $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$: adjacent-depth discrimination does not survive the scale transition, though accuracy remains $2{\\times}$ chance.\nWhether scale-adapted features can recover the signal is an open question that connects depth estimation to the broader model collapse research agenda.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why tails, not means.}\nMean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\\%) because recursive training shifts the bulk distribution only gradually; erosion concentrates in the tails \\cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}, where upper-quantile features capture depth-dependent compression before bulk statistics respond.\nThe strategy effect (9.3\\,pp) exceeding the model effect (7.2\\,pp) implies that auditing pipelines must account for decoding configuration, not just model identity.\n\n\\paragraph{From ceiling to floor.}\nFull-replacement chains provide the discriminability ceiling (d1--d3: 59--82\\%); pilot accumulation experiments ($-$14 to $-$15\\,pp vs.\\ 84.2\\% full-replacement) confirm signal survives partial mixing but attenuates.\nA deployment scenario is depth-scored data curation: score each passage for estimated recursive depth and filter or down-weight high-depth text before it enters the training loop.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale dependence and outlook.}\nAt 14B, $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$: adjacent-depth discrimination does not survive the scale transition, though accuracy remains $2{\\times}$ chance; whether scale-adapted features can recover the signal is open.\nDepth estimation complements binary detection: binary detectors handle the human-vs-machine boundary ($>$97\\% d0 recall) while ordinal features provide finer resolution.",

    "oldString": "At 14B, $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$: adjacent-depth discrimination does not survive the scale transition, though accuracy remains $2{\\times}$ chance.\nWhether scale-adapted features can recover the signal is an open question that connects depth estimation to the broader model collapse research agenda.",
    "newString": "At 14B, $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$: adjacent-depth discrimination does not survive the scale transition, though accuracy remains $2{\\times}$ chance; whether scale-adapted features can recover the signal remains open.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why tails, not means.}\nMean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\\%) because recursive training shifts the bulk distribution only gradually; erosion concentrates in the tails \\cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}, where upper-quantile features capture depth-dependent compression before bulk statistics respond.\nThe strategy effect (9.3\\,pp) exceeding the model effect (7.2\\,pp) implies that auditing pipelines must account for decoding configuration, not just model identity.\n\n\\paragraph{From ceiling to floor.}\nFull-replacement chains provide the discriminability ceiling (d1--d3: 59--82\\%); pilot accumulation experiments ($-$14 to $-$15\\,pp vs.\\ 84.2\\% full-replacement) confirm signal survives partial mixing but attenuates.\nA deployment scenario is depth-scored data curation: score each passage for estimated recursive depth and filter or down-weight high-depth text before it enters the training loop.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale dependence and outlook.}\nAt 14B, $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$: adjacent-depth discrimination does not survive the scale transition, though accuracy remains $2{\\times}$ chance.\nWhether scale-adapted features can recover the signal is an open question that connects depth estimation to the broader model collapse research agenda.",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_016yZeUdjJFnAVdXc4MuTecc`</summary>

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	\paragraph{Full-replacement vs.\ accumulation.}
5	All experiments use full-replacement recursive chains, where each depth fully replaces the prior training corpus. Real-world contamination typically involves accumulation, where synthetic and human data co-exist at unknown proportions across iterations \cite{gerstgrasser2024_accumulate}. Under accumulation, depth signal should attenuate because each generation retains human-originated distributional properties. Our results therefore represent an upper bound on feature separability; extending the framework to accumulation settings, where depth becomes a continuous mixing parameter, is a priority for future work.
6	Segment-level estimation in mixed-depth documents remains open (Appendix~\ref{app:mixed_depth}), as our framework assumes uniform depth per passage.
7	
8	\paragraph{Controlled experimental setting.}
9	The primary benchmark uses Wikipedia seed text with controlled LoRA fine-tuning (1--1.5B models), a design that isolates the depth variable but diverges from wild contamination.
10	Domain bias controls confirm depth as the dominant factor: LOGO accuracy (72.1\%) matches standard CV (72.0\%), seed-group prediction is at chance (10.2\%; Appendix~\ref{app:domain_bias}).
11	Cross-scale experiments (7--14B, \S\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}) show signal that attenuates with scale; at 14B, d0/d1 separability nearly vanishes ($\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$) while d1--d3 discrimination persists; this constitutes a boundary condition for the current feature set (confounded by five factors; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_scale_confounds}).
12	
13	\paragraph{Cross-strategy deployment constraint.}
14	Cross-strategy transfer induces a d1/d2 boundary inversion under temperature sampling.
15	Per-strategy z-score normalization partially corrects the inversion under logistic regression (GPT-2~XL d1v2 AUC $0.24{\to}0.56$, OLMo $0.19{\to}0.70$) but yields negligible improvement for the Random Forest ($\Delta < 0.02$\,pp); a two-stage pipeline is needed for reliable cross-strategy deployment.
16	In strategy-unknown settings, d1--d3 accuracy drops to 45.9\%; the pipeline recovers 11.5\,pp, though gains vary across models (GPT-2~XL $+$16.4\,pp vs.\ Pythia $+$8.0\,pp).
17	
18	\paragraph{d0 inflation of headline metrics.}
19	Depth~0 (human-written text) is near-perfectly separable (recall $>$97\%), inflating four-class accuracy.
20	Cross-model transfer of 66.4\% is substantially driven by d0; d2 recall drops to 26.8\% in the worst case (Pythia holdout).
21	We report d1--d3 balanced accuracy alongside all four-class headlines to address this inflation.
22	
23	\paragraph{Domain-dependent false positive rate.}
24	On editorial text (WikiText-103), all classifiers achieve $\geq$98.5\% d0 accuracy, but on web-crawl data (C4, 5{,}000 passages), per-model FPR ranges from 4.7\% (OLMo) to 22.4\% (GPT-2~XL).
25	A pooled RF classifier evaluated on larger corpora (10{,}000 passages each) yields 34\% FPR on C4 and 17\% on FineWeb \cite{penedo2024_fineweb}; the gap reflects quality filtering in FineWeb removing feature-anomalous text that triggers false positives.
26	Misclassifications concentrate in template/SEO content with elevated repetition features (\texttt{rep\_3gram} $+$37\%, \texttt{rep\_4gram} $+$75\% vs.\ correctly classified d0).
27	Confidence thresholding ($P(\mathrm{d0}) \geq 0.15$) reduces pooled FPR from 34\%/17\% to 2.8\%/0.7\% on C4/FineWeb at minimal accuracy cost ($-$0.3\,pp); high FPR is therefore a decision-threshold problem, not a fundamental calibration defect, though the optimal threshold remains domain-dependent (Appendix~\ref{app:calibration}).
28	
29	\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}
30	Adversarial paraphrasing \cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase} could disrupt the perplexity tail features that drive classification; depth estimation's robustness under such attacks is untested.
31	
32	\paragraph{EDD monotonicity assumption.}
33	Our chains extend to depth~5; deeper iterations (8--60 rounds, as studied in the collapse literature) are expected to converge to indistinguishability based on the observed AUC decay trajectory, but this remains unverified.
34	Our EDD definition (Eq.~\ref{eq:edd}) requires all pairwise AUCs up to depth $d^*$ to exceed $T$, which is well-behaved when AUC decays monotonically, as observed in all our configurations.
35	However, \citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} show that early recursion can temporarily \emph{improve} model quality, which could produce non-monotonic AUC profiles (e.g., a dip at d1/d2 followed by recovery at d2/d3).
36	Our cross-strategy transfer results provide an empirical example: training on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1-vs-d2 AUC as low as 0.129 (GPT-2~XL) and 0.253 (OLMo), violating the monotonicity assumption.
37	In such cases EDD would conservatively report a lower value than the deepest discriminable pair, potentially underestimating the true discriminable range; extending EDD to handle non-contiguous discriminable regions is future work.
38	The choice of threshold $T$ is also application-dependent: at $T{=}0.80$, several cells lose one depth level relative to $T{=}0.75$.
39	
40	\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}
41	Reference model choice affects classification accuracy by up to 11\,pp; same-family references yield optimal performance, though all configurations exceed $2.5\times$ chance.
42	Surprisal-based features are computed relative to Pythia-1.4B, which coincides with one of the three generators.
43	For the Pythia pipeline this constitutes self-model feature extraction, while OLMo and GPT-2~XL measurements are cross-model.
44	The self-reference configuration yields a 4--11\,pp advantage over cross-model references (Table~\ref{tab:ref_ablation}); Pythia results in Table~\ref{tab:main_matrix} ($^\dagger$) should be interpreted with this caveat.
45	An ablation with OLMo-1B and GPT-2 XL as alternative references shows accuracy ranging from 67--79\% across three reference architectures (Appendix~\ref{app:ref_ablation}), all exceeding $2.5\times$ chance; same-family reference yields optimal performance ($\Delta{\leq}$11\,pp).
46	The choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.
47	Reference-free surface features alone achieve 53.7\% (partial signal in lexical statistics) but trail the full pipeline by 25pp; surprisal-based features are therefore essential.
48	Simple multi-reference ensembling does not outperform the best single reference (73.7\% vs.\ 78.6\%).
49	
50	\paragraph{Single LoRA instance.}
51	Cross-seed replication covers 3 of 9 cells (Pythia, OLMo, GPT-2~XL nucleus); seed=43 yields $+$0.1 to $+$3.2\,pp higher accuracy, with 2/3 deltas statistically significant (non-overlapping CIs).
52	Feature rankings and depth ordering are consistent across seeds, but single-seed absolute accuracy carries ${\sim}$1--3\,pp uncertainty; cross-cell comparisons should be interpreted cautiously.
53	
54	\paragraph{Additional caveats.}
55	LoRA rank ($r$) and scaling factor ($\alpha$) sensitivity was not explored; all experiments use $r{=}16$, $\alpha{=}32$.
56	Depth signal scales with passage length (68.4\% at 128 tokens, 77.8\% at 512; Appendix~\ref{app:length_ablation}), saturating near 500 tokens.
57	Prompted generation was validated on only 1 of 9 cells (GPT-2~XL nucleus); generalization to other model--strategy configurations under prompted conditions is not established.
58	Detection features degrade under domain shift \cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and curated synthetic data may not trigger collapse \cite{falahati2026_curated}, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.
59	
60	\section*{Ethics Statement}
61	
62	This work uses publicly available pretrained language models (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and Wikipedia text as seed data.
63	No human subjects were involved, no private or sensitive data were collected, and no new models were released.
64	Our method is intended to support data provenance and training-data auditing; we acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be misused to circumvent content moderation, though such risks are shared with all detection-oriented research.
65	Code and data will be released upon acceptance.
66	This research used AI coding assistants for experiment implementation and writing drafts.
67	All scientific decisions, experimental design, and result interpretation were conducted by the authors.
```

Our results therefore represent an upper bound on feature separability; extending the framework to accumulation settings, where depth becomes a continuous mixing parameter, is a priority for future work.\nSegment-level estimation in mixed-depth documents remains open (Appendix~\\ref{app:mixed_depth}), as our framework assumes uniform depth per passage.\n\n\\paragraph{Controlled experimental setting.}\nThe primary benchmark uses Wikipedia seed text with controlled LoRA fine-tuning (1--1.5B models), a design that isolates the depth variable but diverges from wild contamination.\nDomain bias controls confirm depth as the dominant factor: LOGO accuracy (72.1\\%) matches standard CV (72.0\\%), seed-group prediction is at chance (10.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:domain_bias}).\nCross-scale experiments (7--14B, \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}) show signal that attenuates with scale; at 14B, d0/d1 separability nearly vanishes ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$) while d1--d3 discrimination persists; this constitutes a boundary condition for the current feature set (confounded by five factors; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_confounds}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-strategy deployment constraint.}\nCross-strategy transfer induces a d1/d2 boundary inversion under temperature sampling.\nPer-strategy z-score normalization partially corrects the inversion under logistic regression (GPT-2~XL d1v2 AUC $0.24{\\to}0.56$, OLMo $0.19{\\to}0.70$) but yields negligible improvement for the Random Forest ($\\Delta < 0.02$\\,pp); a two-stage pipeline is needed for reliable cross-strategy deployment.\nIn strategy-unknown settings, d1--d3 accuracy drops to 45.9\\%; the pipeline recovers 11.5\\,pp, though gains vary across models (GPT-2~XL $+$16.4\\,pp vs.\\ Pythia $+$8.0\\,pp).\n\n\\paragraph{d0 inflation of headline metrics.}\nDepth~0 (human-written text) is near-perfectly separable (recall $>$97\\%), inflating four-class accuracy.\nCross-model transfer of 66.4\\% is substantially driven by d0; d2 recall drops to 26.8\\% in the worst case (Pythia holdout).\nWe report d1--d3 balanced accuracy alongside all four-class headlines to address this inflation.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-dependent false positive rate.}\nOn editorial text (WikiText-103), all classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy, but on web-crawl data (C4, 5{,}000 passages), per-model FPR ranges from 4.7\\% (OLMo) to 22.4\\% (GPT-2~XL).\nA pooled RF classifier evaluated on larger corpora (10{,}000 passages each) yields 34\\% FPR on C4 and 17\\% on FineWeb \\cite{penedo2024_fineweb}; the gap reflects quality filtering in FineWeb removing feature-anomalous text that triggers false positives.\nMisclassifications concentrate in template/SEO content with elevated repetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_3gram} $+$37\\%, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram} $+$75\\% vs.\\ correctly classified d0).\nConfidence thresholding ($P(\\mathrm{d0}) \\geq 0.15$) reduces pooled FPR from 34\\%/17\\% to 2.8\\%/0.7\\% on C4/FineWeb at minimal accuracy cost ($-$0.3\\,pp); high FPR is therefore a decision-threshold problem, not a fundamental calibration defect, though the optimal threshold remains domain-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}).\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}\nAdversarial paraphrasing \\cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase} could disrupt the perplexity tail features that drive classification; depth estimation's robustness under such attacks is untested.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD monotonicity assumption.}\nOur chains extend to depth~5; deeper iterations (8--60 rounds, as studied in the collapse literature) are expected to converge to indistinguishability based on the observed AUC decay trajectory, but this remains unverified.\nOur EDD definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}) requires all pairwise AUCs up to depth $d^*$ to exceed $T$, which is well-behaved when AUC decays monotonically, as observed in all our configurations.\nHowever, \\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} show that early recursion can temporarily \\emph{improve} model quality, which could produce non-monotonic AUC profiles (e.g., a dip at d1/d2 followed by recovery at d2/d3).\nOur cross-strategy transfer results provide an empirical example: training on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1-vs-d2 AUC as low as 0.129 (GPT-2~XL) and 0.253 (OLMo), violating the monotonicity assumption.\nIn such cases EDD would conservatively report a lower value than the deepest discriminable pair, potentially underestimating the true discriminable range; extending EDD to handle non-contiguous discriminable regions is future work.\nThe choice of threshold $T$ is also application-dependent: at $T{=}0.80$, several cells lose one depth level relative to $T{=}0.75$.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}\nReference model choice affects classification accuracy by up to 11\\,pp; same-family references yield optimal performance, though all configurations exceed $2.5\\times$ chance.\nSurprisal-based features are computed relative to Pythia-1.4B, which coincides with one of the three generators.\nFor the Pythia pipeline this constitutes self-model feature extraction, while OLMo and GPT-2~XL measurements are cross-model.\nThe self-reference configuration yields a 4--11\\,pp advantage over cross-model references (Table~\\ref{tab:ref_ablation}); Pythia results in Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} ($^\\dagger$) should be interpreted with this caveat.\nAn ablation with OLMo-1B and GPT-2 XL as alternative references shows accuracy ranging from 67--79\\% across three reference architectures (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}), all exceeding $2.5\\times$ chance; same-family reference yields optimal performance ($\\Delta{\\leq}$11\\,pp).\nThe choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.\nReference-free surface features alone achieve 53.7\\% (partial signal in lexical statistics) but trail the full pipeline by 25pp; surprisal-based features are therefore essential.\nSimple multi-reference ensembling does not outperform the best single reference (73.7\\% vs.\\ 78.6\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Single LoRA instance.}\nCross-seed replication covers 3 of 9 cells (Pythia, OLMo, GPT-2~XL nucleus); seed=43 yields $+$0.1 to $+$3.2\\,pp higher accuracy, with 2/3 deltas statistically significant (non-overlapping CIs).\nFeature rankings and depth ordering are consistent across seeds, but single-seed absolute accuracy carries ${\\sim}$1--3\\,pp uncertainty; cross-cell comparisons should be interpreted cautiously.\n\n\\paragraph{Additional caveats.}\nLoRA rank ($r$) and scaling factor ($\\alpha$) sensitivity was not explored; all experiments use $r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$.\nDepth signal scales with passage length (68.4\\% at 128 tokens, 77.8\\% at 512; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_ablation}), saturating near 500 tokens.\nPrompted generation was validated on only 1 of 9 cells (GPT-2~XL nucleus); generalization to other model--strategy configurations under prompted conditions is not established.\nDetection features degrade under domain shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and curated synthetic data may not trigger collapse \\cite{falahati2026_curated}, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available pretrained language models (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and Wikipedia text as seed data.\nNo human subjects were involved, no private or sensitive data were collected, and no new models were released.\nOur method is intended to support data provenance and training-data auditing; we acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be misused to circumvent content moderation, though such risks are shared with all detection-oriented research.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\nThis research used AI coding assistants for experiment implementation and writing drafts.\nAll scientific decisions, experimental design, and result interpretation were conducted by the authors.",

a two-stage pipeline is needed for reliable cross-strategy deployment.\nIn strategy-unknown settings, d1--d3 accuracy drops to 45.9\\%; the pipeline recovers 11.5\\,pp, though gains vary across models (GPT-2~XL $+$16.4\\,pp vs.\\ Pythia $+$8.0\\,pp).\n\n\\paragraph{d0 inflation of headline metrics.}\nDepth~0 (human-written text) is near-perfectly separable (recall $>$97\\%), inflating four-class accuracy.\nCross-model transfer of 66.4\\% is substantially driven by d0; d2 recall drops to 26.8\\% in the worst case (Pythia holdout).\nWe report d1--d3 balanced accuracy alongside all four-class headlines to address this inflation.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-dependent false positive rate.}\nOn editorial text (WikiText-103), all classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy, but on web-crawl data (C4, 5{,}000 passages), per-model FPR ranges from 4.7\\% (OLMo) to 22.4\\% (GPT-2~XL).\nA pooled RF classifier evaluated on larger corpora (10{,}000 passages each) yields 34\\% FPR on C4 and 17\\% on FineWeb \\cite{penedo2024_fineweb}; the gap reflects quality filtering in FineWeb removing feature-anomalous text that triggers false positives.\nMisclassifications concentrate in template/SEO content with elevated repetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_3gram} $+$37\\%, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram} $+$75\\% vs.\\ correctly classified d0).\nConfidence thresholding ($P(\\mathrm{d0}) \\geq 0.15$) reduces pooled FPR from 34\\%/17\\% to 2.8\\%/0.7\\% on C4/FineWeb at minimal accuracy cost ($-$0.3\\,pp); high FPR is therefore a decision-threshold problem, not a fundamental calibration defect, though the optimal threshold remains domain-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}).\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}\nAdversarial paraphrasing \\cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase} could disrupt the perplexity tail features that drive classification; depth estimation's robustness under such attacks is untested.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD monotonicity assumption.}\nOur chains extend to depth~5; deeper iterations (8--60 rounds, as studied in the collapse literature) are expected to converge to indistinguishability based on the observed AUC decay trajectory, but this remains unverified.\nOur EDD definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}) requires all pairwise AUCs up to depth $d^*$ to exceed $T$, which is well-behaved when AUC decays monotonically, as observed in all our configurations.\nHowever, \\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} show that early recursion can temporarily \\emph{improve} model quality, which could produce non-monotonic AUC profiles (e.g., a dip at d1/d2 followed by recovery at d2/d3).\nOur cross-strategy transfer results provide an empirical example: training on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1-vs-d2 AUC as low as 0.129 (GPT-2~XL) and 0.253 (OLMo), violating the monotonicity assumption.\nIn such cases EDD would conservatively report a lower value than the deepest discriminable pair, potentially underestimating the true discriminable range; extending EDD to handle non-contiguous discriminable regions is future work.\nThe choice of threshold $T$ is also application-dependent: at $T{=}0.80$, several cells lose one depth level relative to $T{=}0.75$.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}\nReference model choice affects classification accuracy by up to 11\\,pp; same-family references yield optimal performance, though all configurations exceed $2.5\\times$ chance.\nSurprisal-based features are computed relative to Pythia-1.4B, which coincides with one of the three generators.\nFor the Pythia pipeline this constitutes self-model feature extraction, while OLMo and GPT-2~XL measurements are cross-model.\nThe self-reference configuration yields a 4--11\\,pp advantage over cross-model references (Table~\\ref{tab:ref_ablation}); Pythia results in Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} ($^\\dagger$) should be interpreted with this caveat.\nAn ablation with OLMo-1B and GPT-2 XL as alternative references shows accuracy ranging from 67--79\\% across three reference architectures (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}), all exceeding $2.5\\times$ chance; same-family reference yields optimal performance ($\\Delta{\\leq}$11\\,pp).\nThe choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.\nReference-free surface features alone achieve 53.7\\% (partial signal in lexical statistics) but trail the full pipeline by 25pp; surprisal-based features are therefore essential.\nSimple multi-reference ensembling does not outperform the best single reference (73.7\\% vs.\\ 78.6\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Single LoRA instance.}\nCross-seed replication covers 3 of 9 cells (Pythia, OLMo, GPT-2~XL nucleus); seed=43 yields $+$0.1 to $+$3.2\\,pp higher accuracy, with 2/3 deltas statistically significant (non-overlapping CIs).\nFeature rankings and depth ordering are consistent across seeds, but single-seed absolute accuracy carries ${\\sim}$1--3\\,pp uncertainty; cross-cell comparisons should be interpreted cautiously.\n\n\\paragraph{Additional caveats.}\nLoRA rank ($r$) and scaling factor ($\\alpha$) sensitivity was not explored; all experiments use $r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$.\nDepth signal scales with passage length (68.4\\% at 128 tokens, 77.8\\% at 512; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_ablation}), saturating near 500 tokens.\nPrompted generation was validated on only 1 of 9 cells (GPT-2~XL nucleus); generalization to other model--strategy configurations under prompted conditions is not established.\nDetection features degrade under domain shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and curated synthetic data may not trigger collapse \\cite{falahati2026_curated}, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Full-replacement vs.\\ accumulation.}\nAll experiments use full-replacement recursive chains, where each depth fully replaces the prior training corpus.\nReal-world contamination typically involves accumulation, where synthetic and human data co-exist at unknown proportions \\cite{gerstgrasser2024_accumulate}.\nPilot accumulation experiments show 14--15\\,pp attenuation relative to full-replacement; our results therefore represent an upper bound on feature separability, and extending the framework to accumulation settings is a priority for future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale and generalization boundaries.}\nThe primary benchmark uses 1--1.5B parameter models with controlled LoRA fine-tuning.\nAt 14B, adjacent-depth discrimination does not survive the scale transition ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$), constituting a boundary condition for the current feature set.\nCross-strategy transfer drops d1--d3 accuracy to 45.9\\%, and on web-crawl data (C4) false positive rates reach 22\\%; these gaps narrow with confidence thresholding and strategy-aware pipelines but are not fully resolved.\nAdditional caveats---reference model dependency (${\\leq}$11\\,pp), single LoRA seed (${\\sim}$1--3\\,pp uncertainty), adversarial robustness, and passage length sensitivity---are detailed in the Appendix.",
    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Full-replacement vs.\\ accumulation.}\nAll experiments use full-replacement recursive chains, where each depth fully replaces the prior training corpus. Real-world contamination typically involves accumulation, where synthetic and human data co-exist at unknown proportions across iterations \\cite{gerstgrasser2024_accumulate}. Under accumulation, depth signal should attenuate because each generation retains human-originated distributional properties. Our results therefore represent an upper bound on feature separability; extending the framework to accumulation settings, where depth becomes a continuous mixing parameter, is a priority for future work.\nSegment-level estimation in mixed-depth documents remains open (Appendix~\\ref{app:mixed_depth}), as our framework assumes uniform depth per passage.\n\n\\paragraph{Controlled experimental setting.}\nThe primary benchmark uses Wikipedia seed text with controlled LoRA fine-tuning (1--1.5B models), a design that isolates the depth variable but diverges from wild contamination.\nDomain bias controls confirm depth as the dominant factor: LOGO accuracy (72.1\\%) matches standard CV (72.0\\%), seed-group prediction is at chance (10.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:domain_bias}).\nCross-scale experiments (7--14B, \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}) show signal that attenuates with scale; at 14B, d0/d1 separability nearly vanishes ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$) while d1--d3 discrimination persists; this constitutes a boundary condition for the current feature set (confounded by five factors; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_confounds}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-strategy deployment constraint.}\nCross-strategy transfer induces a d1/d2 boundary inversion under temperature sampling.\nPer-strategy z-score normalization partially corrects the inversion under logistic regression (GPT-2~XL d1v2 AUC $0.24{\\to}0.56$, OLMo $0.19{\\to}0.70$) but yields negligible improvement for the Random Forest ($\\Delta < 0.02$\\,pp); a two-stage pipeline is needed for reliable cross-strategy deployment.\nIn strategy-unknown settings, d1--d3 accuracy drops to 45.9\\%; the pipeline recovers 11.5\\,pp, though gains vary across models (GPT-2~XL $+$16.4\\,pp vs.\\ Pythia $+$8.0\\,pp).\n\n\\paragraph{d0 inflation of headline metrics.}\nDepth~0 (human-written text) is near-perfectly separable (recall $>$97\\%), inflating four-class accuracy.\nCross-model transfer of 66.4\\% is substantially driven by d0; d2 recall drops to 26.8\\% in the worst case (Pythia holdout).\nWe report d1--d3 balanced accuracy alongside all four-class headlines to address this inflation.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-dependent false positive rate.}\nOn editorial text (WikiText-103), all classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy, but on web-crawl data (C4, 5{,}000 passages), per-model FPR ranges from 4.7\\% (OLMo) to 22.4\\% (GPT-2~XL).\nA pooled RF classifier evaluated on larger corpora (10{,}000 passages each) yields 34\\% FPR on C4 and 17\\% on FineWeb \\cite{penedo2024_fineweb}; the gap reflects quality filtering in FineWeb removing feature-anomalous text that triggers false positives.\nMisclassifications concentrate in template/SEO content with elevated repetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_3gram} $+$37\\%, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram} $+$75\\% vs.\\ correctly classified d0).\nConfidence thresholding ($P(\\mathrm{d0}) \\geq 0.15$) reduces pooled FPR from 34\\%/17\\% to 2.8\\%/0.7\\% on C4/FineWeb at minimal accuracy cost ($-$0.3\\,pp); high FPR is therefore a decision-threshold problem, not a fundamental calibration defect, though the optimal threshold remains domain-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}).\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}\nAdversarial paraphrasing \\cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase} could disrupt the perplexity tail features that drive classification; depth estimation's robustness under such attacks is untested.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD monotonicity assumption.}\nOur chains extend to depth~5; deeper iterations (8--60 rounds, as studied in the collapse literature) are expected to converge to indistinguishability based on the observed AUC decay trajectory, but this remains unverified.\nOur EDD definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}) requires all pairwise AUCs up to depth $d^*$ to exceed $T$, which is well-behaved when AUC decays monotonically, as observed in all our configurations.\nHowever, \\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} show that early recursion can temporarily \\emph{improve} model quality, which could produce non-monotonic AUC profiles (e.g., a dip at d1/d2 followed by recovery at d2/d3).\nOur cross-strategy transfer results provide an empirical example: training on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1-vs-d2 AUC as low as 0.129 (GPT-2~XL) and 0.253 (OLMo), violating the monotonicity assumption.\nIn such cases EDD would conservatively report a lower value than the deepest discriminable pair, potentially underestimating the true discriminable range; extending EDD to handle non-contiguous discriminable regions is future work.\nThe choice of threshold $T$ is also application-dependent: at $T{=}0.80$, several cells lose one depth level relative to $T{=}0.75$.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}\nReference model choice affects classification accuracy by up to 11\\,pp; same-family references yield optimal performance, though all configurations exceed $2.5\\times$ chance.\nSurprisal-based features are computed relative to Pythia-1.4B, which coincides with one of the three generators.\nFor the Pythia pipeline this constitutes self-model feature extraction, while OLMo and GPT-2~XL measurements are cross-model.\nThe self-reference configuration yields a 4--11\\,pp advantage over cross-model references (Table~\\ref{tab:ref_ablation}); Pythia results in Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} ($^\\dagger$) should be interpreted with this caveat.\nAn ablation with OLMo-1B and GPT-2 XL as alternative references shows accuracy ranging from 67--79\\% across three reference architectures (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}), all exceeding $2.5\\times$ chance; same-family reference yields optimal performance ($\\Delta{\\leq}$11\\,pp).\nThe choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.\nReference-free surface features alone achieve 53.7\\% (partial signal in lexical statistics) but trail the full pipeline by 25pp; surprisal-based features are therefore essential.\nSimple multi-reference ensembling does not outperform the best single reference (73.7\\% vs.\\ 78.6\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Single LoRA instance.}\nCross-seed replication covers 3 of 9 cells (Pythia, OLMo, GPT-2~XL nucleus); seed=43 yields $+$0.1 to $+$3.2\\,pp higher accuracy, with 2/3 deltas statistically significant (non-overlapping CIs).\nFeature rankings and depth ordering are consistent across seeds, but single-seed absolute accuracy carries ${\\sim}$1--3\\,pp uncertainty; cross-cell comparisons should be interpreted cautiously.\n\n\\paragraph{Additional caveats.}\nLoRA rank ($r$) and scaling factor ($\\alpha$) sensitivity was not explored; all experiments use $r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$.\nDepth signal scales with passage length (68.4\\% at 128 tokens, 77.8\\% at 512; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_ablation}), saturating near 500 tokens.\nPrompted generation was validated on only 1 of 9 cells (GPT-2~XL nucleus); generalization to other model--strategy configurations under prompted conditions is not established.\nDetection features degrade under domain shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and curated synthetic data may not trigger collapse \\cite{falahati2026_curated}, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available pretrained language models (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and Wikipedia text as seed data.\nNo human subjects were involved, no private or sensitive data were collected, and no new models were released.\nOur method is intended to support data provenance and training-data auditing; we acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be misused to circumvent content moderation, though such risks are shared with all detection-oriented research.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\nThis research used AI coding assistants for experiment implementation and writing drafts.\nAll scientific decisions, experimental design, and result interpretation were conducted by the authors.",
